Coronavirus (COVID-19) cases, %%COUNTRY%% data Source: World Health Organization Loading... Loading... Loading... What is the definition of 'confirmed' and 'reported' in COVID-19 data? All data represents the date of reporting as opposed to date of symptom onset. All data is subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected at more frequent intervals with some countries performing retrospective bulk corrections may lead to the appearance of significant spikes or negative values which are verified and validated by WHO. In the context of WHO's statistical reporting of COVID-19 data, it is important to note that only confirmed cases are included in case and death counts. In guidance updated on 22 July 2023; there are two alternative definitions for 'Confirmed case of SARS-CoV-2 infection' in international surveillance reporting, although some departures may exist due to local adaptations: a) A person with a positive Nucleic Acid Amplification Test (NAAT), regardless of clinical criteria OR epidemiological criteria. b) A person meeting clinical criteria AND/OR epidemiological criteria (suspect case A) with a positive professional-use or self-test SARS-CoV-2 Antigen-RDT. Additionally, WHO only disseminates data as reported by its Member States. From the 31 December 2019 to the 21 March 2020, WHO collected the numbers of confirmed COVID-19 cases and deaths through official communications under the International Health Regulations (IHR, 2005), complemented by monitoring the official ministries of health websites and social media accounts. Since 22 March 2020, global data has been compiled through WHO region-specific dashboards, and/or aggregate count data reported directly to WHO headquarters by Member States. Statistical counts include both domestic and repatriated cases. Case detection, definitions, testing strategies, reporting practice, and lag times (e.g. time to case notification, and time to reporting of deaths) differ between countries, territories and areas. These factors, amongst others, influence the counts presented with variable under or overestimation of true case and death counts, and variable delays to reflecting these statistics at a global level. Please note that statistics do not necessarily reflect the actual number of cases and deaths or the actual number of countries where cases and deaths are occurring, as a number of countries have stopped reporting or changed their frequency of reporting. Trends in COVID-19 cases, %%COUNTRY%% Recent COVID-19 cases reported to WHO (weekly) %%COUNTRY%%, July 2023 - present Source: World Health Organization Data may be incomplete for the latest week. Total COVID-19 cases reported to WHO (weekly) %%COUNTRY%%, January 2020 - present Source: World Health Organization Why is COVID-19 data being presented as weekly statistics? A number of countries have stopped reporting or changed their frequency of reporting COVID-19 case and death counts to WHO. An outcome of these differences in reporting is that WHO may receive daily data from some countries, while other countries may only report data to WHO once every 14 days. In addition, countries differ in how they choose to report statistics; some countries provide their data attributed to specific dates while others who report less frequently may group data from 7 days into a single statistic attributed to a week in their reporting. As of 25 August 2023, WHO declared that it is no longer necessary for Member States to report daily counts of cases and deaths to WHO and requested strengthening of weekly reporting. Reported data is still available attributed to specific dates (daily data) here as a download. This dashboard presents the same statistics as weekly figures in charts in order to mitigate against the visual misinterpretation of data. Should daily data have been presented here, many countries would show zero counts for multiple consecutive days due in part to the differences in how they choose to report. While weekly intervals do not completely mitigate against this, the approach reduces the risk that some dashboard users might infer zero cases or deaths when lack of data is often due to reporting differences. What do negative counts of cases or deaths mean? All data is subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected by Member States at more frequent intervals with some countries performing retrospective bulk corrections in their reporting. Bulk corrections may lead to the appearance of significant spikes or negative values which are verified and validated by WHO. Last 28 days ‘Last 28 days’ refers to the four preceding most recently complete epidemiological weeks. WHO Regions The World Health Organization (WHO) is divided into six regions with regional offices, each responsible for addressing health challenges and promoting public health within its designated geographical area. These regions serve as administrative units that allow the WHO to tailor its efforts to the specific needs, priorities, and health conditions of member countries within each region. World Bank Income Groups The World Bank classifies economies into income groups based on their Gross National Income (GNI) per capita. These annually updated classifications help the World Bank and other organizations analyze and understand global economic trends, allocate resources, and design development policies. Data sources COVID-19 case data From the 31 December 2019 to the 21 March 2020, WHO collected the numbers of confirmed COVID-19 cases and deaths through official communications under the International Health Regulations (IHR, 2005), complemented by monitoring the official ministries of health websites and social media accounts. Since 22 March 2020, global data is compiled through WHO region-specific dashboards, and/or aggregate count data reported to WHO headquarters. WHO COVID-19 Dashboard is updated every Tuesday for the period of two full weeks prior. Counts primarily reflect laboratory-confirmed cases and deaths, based upon WHO case definitions; although some departures may exist due to local adaptations. Counts include both domestic and repatriated cases. Case detection, definitions, testing strategies, reporting practice, and lag times (e.g. time to case notification, and time to reporting of deaths) differ between countries, territories and areas. These factors, amongst others, influence the counts presented with variable under or overestimation of true case and death counts, and variable delays to reflecting these data at a global level. All data represent date of reporting as opposed to date of symptom onset. All data are subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected at more frequent intervals. Data have been included retrospectively since 31 July 2023. Rates <0.001 per 100,000 population may be rounded to 0. Time
From 31 December 2019 to 21 March 2020, and since 22 March 2020, with weekly updates from July 2023 onwards (N14, N15, N21).
Location and population
Global, with data collected from countries, territories, and areas, including high, low, lower middle, and upper middle income groups (N1, N16).
Event
WHO monitors and reports on the global epidemiological situation of SARS-CoV-2 through the WHO COVID-19 dashboard, including confirmed cases and deaths (N1, N4, N10).
Scale, impact and risk
The dashboard includes confirmed cases and deaths reported to WHO, with data subject to verification and potential changes due to retrospective updates or changes in reporting practices (N1, N8, N18).
Response, status and uncertainty
WHO collects and disseminates data as reported by its Member States, with potential for significant data errors and corrections, and acknowledges differences in case detection, testing strategies, and reporting practices across regions (N13, N9, N18).

呼吸道病毒活动水平 - 美国疾病控制与预防中心 | CDC (.gov)

Respiratory Virus Activity Levels - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)
新闻简报美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 于 2026 年 9 月 25 日发布了呼吸道病毒活动水平的最新信息。该报告包含有关 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 和相关病毒(例如流行性乙型冠状病毒)的一般信息,但没有提供这些实体的具体病例数、死亡人数或风险级别。疾病预防控制中心强调了公共卫生措施和预防策略的重要性,但没有报告详细的调查或应对行动。内容是呼吸道病毒活动的总体概述,并不关注任何特定的 暴发 或已确认的事件。该报告是疾病预防控制中心持续努力监测和提供传染病指导的一部分。

A high priorityPublic-health news

Respiratory Virus Activity Levels - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)

News Brief
The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) published an update on respiratory virus activity levels on September 25, 2026. The report includes general information about SARS-CoV-2 and related viruses, such as Betacoronavirus pandemicum, but does not provide specific case counts, death counts, or risk levels for these entities. The CDC emphasized the importance of public health measures and prevention strategies, but no detailed investigation or response actions were reported. The content is a general overview of respiratory virus activity and does not focus on any specific outbreak or confirmed event. The report is part of the CDC's ongoing efforts to monitor and provide guidance on infectious diseases.

新闻简报This Britannica article provides an overview of different types of COVID-19 vaccines, including mRNA, viral-vector, protein-subunit, and inactivated vaccines. It highlights the effectiveness of the Moderna and Pfizer-BioNTech vaccines at 95%, and the Janssen vaccine at 67%. The article does not report any specific case counts or deaths, focusing instead on vaccine types and effectiveness.

Britannica Editors Last updated Sep. 25, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Table of Contents Table of Contents Ask Anything Top Questions What are the different types of COVID-19 vaccines? There are several different types of COVID-19 vaccines. Among these are mRNA vaccines, which deliver genetic instructions that prompt cells to produce the viral spike protein on SARS-CoV-2. Viral-vector vaccines use a harmless modified virus to carry the same instructions to cells. Protein-subunit vaccines supply pieces of the spike protein directly, while inactivated vaccines use whole viruses that have been rendered noninfectious. Each type teaches the immune system to identify and respond to SARS-CoV-2 before illness can occur. How effective are the Moderna and Pfizer-BioNTech vaccines? The Moderna and Pfizer-BioNTech vaccines were about 95 percent effective in preventing infection with SARS-CoV-2. What is the Janssen COVID-19 vaccine’s effectiveness? The Janssen COVID-19 vaccine was about 67 percent effective in preventing moderate to severe disease caused by SARS-CoV-2 infection. What is the recommended COVID-19 vaccine dosage schedule? For the 2025–2026 season, most people are advised to receive one updated COVID-19 vaccine. Children 6 to 23 months may need multiple doses if they are not yet vaccinated, while older children and adults up to age 64 typically require only a single dose. Adults 65 and older are recommended to receive two updated doses. News • States can now access covid vaccines for low-income children, after delay • Sep. 24, 2026, 12:37 AM ET (Washington Post) ...(Show more) New COVID vaccines are coming. A federal power struggle could keep them from you • Aug. 28, 2026, 1:06 PM ET (Deseret News) Show less COVID-19 vaccine, any of various suspensions that contain either modified messenger RNA (mRNA), recombinant proteins, or immune-stimulating components of severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2), the cause of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19). The vaccine, administered by intramuscular injection, is intended to prevent COVID-19, which is characterized mainly by fever and respiratory symptoms, such as difficulty breathing. The first COVID-19 vaccines were developed and made available to the public under emergency use authorization in 2020, amid the COVID-19 pandemic. Quick Facts Target: SARS-CoV-2 Type: mRNA vaccine Year introduced: 2020 Notes: The development of COVID-19 vaccines drew on decades of work, including foundational mRNA research carried out by Katalin Karikó and Drew Weissman. In 2010 Karikó and Weissman licensed their modified mRNA technology to CellScript, which then later sublicencsed it to Pfizer-BioNTech and Moderna. This proved critical during the COVID-19 pandemic. With global funding and parallel clinical trials, effective vaccines targeting the spike protein on SARS-CoV-2 were developed and authorized for emergency use in less than a year. Moderna COVID-19 vaccineVial containing Moderna COVID-19 vaccine, one of the first mRNA vaccines to be approved for use in humans.(more) Multiple COVID-19 vaccines have been developed. Some of the first agents included mRNA-based vaccines made by the pharmaceutical and biotechnology companies Pfizer, Inc., and BioNTech and by the pharmaceutical company Moderna, as well as a recombinant version, known as the Janssen COVID-19 vaccine, developed by Johnson & Johnson. The Moderna and Pfizer-BioNTech vaccines were about 95 percent effective in preventing infection with SARS-CoV-2; the Janssen vaccine was about 67 percent effective in preventing moderate to severe disease. Other vaccines included the Oxford-AstraZeneca COVID-19 vaccine, a recombinant vaccine first approved in the United Kingdom in late 2020, and the Novavax COVID-19 vaccine, a protein-based vaccine that initially was made available in Europe in 2021. Read more about the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. How We Develop New VaccinesWorried about diphtheria? No? Thank vaccines.(more) See all videos for this article Protection against COVID-19 was provided by two doses of any of the different types of vaccines, given three to fours weeks apart; an exception was the single-dose Janssen vaccine. For healthy persons aged 12 and older, two doses, followed at least five months later by a booster dose, offered maximal protection; a booster was recommended two months after the single-dose Janssen vaccine. However, as the COVID-19 pandemic continued into 2022, the effectiveness of the vaccines was found to wane over time, warranting consideration of additional booster shots. In early 2022 the U.S. Food and Drug Administration approved the use of a second booster of Moderna or Pfizer-BioNTech for individuals aged 50 or older, persons with moderate or severe immune dysfunction, and persons who had received a single dose and booster of the Janssen vaccine. COVID-19 vaccines were effective not only in preventing severe illness but also in limiting the spread of SARS-CoV-2. The vaccines also generally were safe. The most common side effects were pain, redness, and swelling at the site of injection. Some individuals also experienced headache, fatigue, and fever; in very rare instances, vaccination was followed by a severe allergic reaction. Side effects could be experienced after any of the doses, including following a booster shot. Britannica Editors Last updated Aug. 1, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Table of Contents Table of Contents Ask Anything Top Questions What is an mRNA vaccine? An mRNA vaccine uses a synthetic version of messenger RNA to instruct cells to produce a particular protein that trains the immune system to recognize and destroy the targeted disease-associated entity. How do mRNA vaccines differ from traditional vaccines? Vaccines based on mRNA use synthetic technologies, without any need for live pathogen particles. This differs from traditional vaccines, which typically use weakened or inactivated pathogens to prompt an immune response. What were the first mRNA vaccines approved for human use? The first mRNA vaccines approved for human use were the COVID-19 vaccines developed by Pfizer-BioNTech and Moderna in 2020. News • New 'smart' nanoparticles help the immune system better attack tumors • Sep. 11, 2026, 2:00 PM ET (Medical Xpress) ...(Show more) New method based on human blood simplifies mRNA drug development • Sep. 1, 2026, 12:10 PM ET (News-Medical) Show less mRNA vaccine, type of vaccine that uses a synthetic version of a molecule known as messenger RNA (mRNA) to instruct cells in the body to produce a specific protein that trains the immune system to recognize and destroy the targeted disease-associated entity. Vaccines based on mRNA rely primarily on synthetic technologies, without any need for actual pathogen particles. This differs from traditional vaccines, which use a weakened or inactivated form of a pathogen to prompt an immune response that provides protection against future infections. The first mRNA vaccines approved for use in humans were the COVID-19 vaccines developed by Pfizer-BioNTech and Moderna; these agents were made available under emergency-use authorization in late 2020, during the COVID-19 pandemic, to protect against infection with severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2). Quick Facts Target: Various pathogens Technology: Leverages synthetic messenger RNA (mRNA) to encode benign protein fragments of pathogens. Year introduced: 2020 Notable mRNA vaccines: COVID-19 vaccines Historical developments How We Develop New VaccinesWorried about diphtheria? No? Thank vaccines.(more) See all videos for this article The idea for employing mRNA in therapeutics emerged in the latter part of the 20th century. Indeed, following the discovery of mRNA in 1961, there was great interest in the potential of using mRNA as a tool for directing cells to produce specific proteins. The development of mRNA-based treatments, however, was hindered by properties inherent to mRNA, particularly its instability and susceptibility to rapid degradation. In the late 1980s researchers demonstrated that vesicles known as liposomal nanoparticles could be used to package and effectively deliver mRNA to cells. This breakthrough marked a significant advance toward the production of stable mRNA vaccines. In the following decade the first mRNA vaccines for influenza were investigated in mice, and by the early 2000s mRNA vaccines were under development to target certain types of cancer cells. Nonetheless, progress remained slow until 2020, when the COVID-19 pandemic struck. A vaccine to help prevent or reduce the severity of SARS-CoV-2 infection was urgently needed. Because mRNA vaccines can be generated quickly, relative to other types of vaccines, scientists had experimental agents prepared for testing within months. Back to homepage By clicking "Sign Up", you consent to the Britannica Privacy Policy and Terms of Use. Mechanism and delivery The development of mRNA vaccines leverages the basic function of mRNA, which is to carry specific instructions from DNA in the cell nucleus to ribosomes in the cell cytoplasm. Ribosomes serve as sites of protein synthesis, where mRNA sequences are read and used to assemble proteins. For mRNA vaccines, this natural process is harnessed using synthetic mRNA to encode a benign protein fragment of the pathogen; an example is the spike protein of SARS-CoV-2. The protein fragment is then displayed on the cell surface, where it is detected by the immune system, which responds by producing antibodies that can then provide protection against later exposure to the actual pathogen. There are several ways in which synthetic mRNA can be delivered to cells. Examples include encapsulation by carriers such as lipid nanoparticles and targeted entry into dendritic cells, which serve as antigen-presenting cells that help initiate immunity. The vaccines may also be carrier-free, wherein naked mRNA is dissolved into solution for direct injection into tissues such as muscle. Advantages and limitations Vaccines that employ synthetic mRNA hold significant promise for addressing various diseases, including bird flu, HIV/AIDS, malaria, and tuberculosis. Vaccines employing synthetic mRNA have key advantages over older vaccine technologies, particularly because their development eliminates the need to grow live viruses or other biological components in the laboratory, which can be time-consuming and complex. In addition, mRNA vaccines are highly adaptable; the mRNA sequence can be quickly modified to address new variants of a virus or entirely different pathogens. The risk of long-term side effects from mRNA vaccines is minimal, owing to their rapid breakdown and removal from the body. In general, mRNA vaccines have improved safety profiles, relative to older types of vaccines, though short-term side effects, such as headache and fatigue, may still occur. Limitations such as lack of stability, cold storage needs, and high production costs, are being overcome through ongoing research. Explore Britannica Premium! COVID-19 Delta variant | virus variant - Britannica

COVID-19 Delta variant | virus variant - Britannica
新闻简报严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 的 Delta 变种于 2021 年中期广泛传播,与该病毒的早期形式相比,它的传播性更高。它导致感染激增,包括在接种疫苗的人群中,并导致严重疾病,特别是在未接种疫苗的人群中。在澳大利亚,政府重新将预防工作重点放在疫苗接种上,该措施因行动缓慢而受到批评。在加拿大,Delta 变种席卷了全国,促使战略转向 疫苗接种,而特鲁多则批评奥图尔提倡自愿测试方法。在印度,这种变种让医疗系统不堪重负,导致莫迪因忽视病毒激增而受到批评。伊朗的应对重点转向了疫苗接种和经济措施。证据来自大英百科全书的文章,但由于人工智能生成的摘要而存在一些局限性。该分析基于已确认的 Delta 变种的传播和影响,没有对其他相关病毒或实体做出任何声明。
查看新闻五要素
时间
Delta 变种于 2021 年中期广泛传播,与早期病毒形式相比,其传播性更强。这场浪潮在五月达到顶峰,但在六月的大部分时间里继续摧毁该国。2021 年 7 月,莫里森提出了一个四阶段战略。后来,在 2021 年底,Omicron 变种迅速成为该病毒的主要形式。
地点与对象
澳大利亚、加拿大、印度和伊朗。Delta 变种引起感染(包括在接种疫苗的个体中)和严重疾病(尤其是在未接种疫苗的个体中)。它使印度的医疗保健系统不堪重负。
事件
2021 年 7 月,莫里森将政府的预防工作重点重新转向 疫苗接种。
规模、影响与风险
在加拿大,Delta变种正在席卷全国,政府将战略转向疫苗接种。
应对、状态与不确定性
澳大利亚政府将预防工作重新集中到疫苗接种上,但该措施被批评行动缓慢。在加拿大,政府将策略转向“疫苗接种”,而特鲁多批评奥图尔主张基于测试而不是“疫苗接种”的自愿应对。在印度,三角洲变种让医疗保健系统不堪重负,莫迪因忽视激增而受到批评。伊朗政府应对措施的重点转向了疫苗接种和经济措施。

COVID-19 Delta variant | virus variant - Britannica

News Brief
The Delta variant of SARS-CoV-2 became widespread in mid-2021, noted for its increased transmissibility compared to earlier forms of the virus. It led to a surge in infections, including among vaccinated individuals, and caused significant illness, particularly in unvaccinated populations. In Australia, the government refocused prevention efforts on vaccination, which was criticized for being slow. In Canada, the Delta variant swept the country, prompting a shift in strategy toward vaccination, while Trudeau criticized O’Toole for advocating a voluntary testing approach. In India, the variant overwhelmed the healthcare system, leading to criticism of Modi for neglecting the surge. The focus of Iran’s response shifted to vaccination and economic measures. The evidence is sourced from Britannica articles, with some limitations noted due to AI-generated summaries. The analysis is grounded in the confirmed spread and impact of the Delta variant, with no claims made about other related viruses or entities.

Antibodies May Not Be Enough: T Cells Emerge as Key Players in Household COVID-19 Transmission - Global Biodefense

News Brief
The article published on September 22, 2026, by Global Biodefense discusses a scientific study suggesting that T cells, rather than antibodies, may be key in household transmission of SARS-CoV-2. This finding challenges the assumption that high antibody levels alone determine susceptibility to infection. The study is part of a broader exploration into immune responses to the virus, highlighting the need for further research. No specific outbreak data, case counts, or public health responses are provided in the evidence. The article emphasizes the importance of understanding T cell dynamics in transmission, which could influence future prevention strategies. However, the findings remain preliminary and are not yet confirmed as a public health event.

重叠的新冠病毒后病症:由连续 EB 病毒 (EBV) 和严重急性呼吸系统综合症冠状病毒 2 (严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2) 感染引发的肌痛性脑脊髓炎和体位性心动过速综合症一例 - Cureus

Overlapping Post-COVID Conditions: A Case of Myalgic Encephalomyelitis and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome Triggered by Sequential Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV) and Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) Infections - Cureus
新闻简报本报告描述了一名 48 岁女性病例,她于 2022 年 1 月感染 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 后出现肌痛性脑脊髓炎/慢性疲劳综合征 (ME/CFS) 和体位性心动过速综合征 (POTS)。她既往有 Epstein-Barr 病毒 (EBV) 单核细胞增多症病史。患者出现持续的致残症状,包括极度疲劳、直立不耐受、感觉异常、认知功能障碍和多系统表现。这些症状严重影响了她的生活质量和功能能力。她于 2023 年初被诊断出患有 ME/CFS,并于同年晚些时候被诊断出患有 POTS。该病例凸显了新冠肺炎后病症 (PCC) 的复杂性,其特点是影响多个器官系统的各种新的、复发的或持续的症状。该报告强调了对 诊断 和管理采取多学科方法的必要性,以及确定这些病症的客观生物标志物的挑战。该研究还强调了持续研究和临床培训的重要性,以更好地解决 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 感染的长期影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该患者在2022年1月感染严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2后出现症状,于2023年初出现ME/CFS症状,同年晚些时候出现POTS症状。发表日期为2026-09-24。
地点与对象
该病例是在临床环境中报告的,患者是一名 48 岁女性,童年有单核细胞增多症病史。证据中没有指明具体地点。
事件
一例 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 感染后出现肌痛性脑脊髓炎/慢性疲劳综合征 (ME/CFS) 和体位性心动过速综合征 (POTS) 的患者,既往有 Epstein-Barr 病毒 (EBV) 单核细胞增多症病史。这是该文章作者报道的一个确诊的临床病例。
规模、影响与风险
患者出现持续性和致残症状,包括极度疲劳、直立不耐受、感觉异常、认知功能障碍和多系统症状。这些情况严重影响了她的生活质量和功能能力。该报告没有提供具体病例或死亡人数,但强调了新冠疫情后状况的复杂性和严重性。
应对、状态与不确定性
患者接受了多学科治疗方法,包括药物治疗和活动调整。诊断 通过临床评估得到证实,该病例作为诊断和管理新冠肺炎后病情挑战的一个例子。调查范围广泛,但没有客观测试证实所有假设,包括肥大细胞激活综合征(MCAS)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Overlapping Post-COVID Conditions: A Case of Myalgic Encephalomyelitis and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome Triggered by Sequential Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV) and Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) Infections - Cureus

News Brief
This report describes a case of a 48-year-old woman who developed Myalgic Encephalomyelitis/Chronic Fatigue Syndrome (ME/CFS) and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome (POTS) following a SARS-CoV-2 infection in January 2022. She had a prior history of Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) mononucleosis. The patient experienced persistent and disabling symptoms, including extreme fatigue, orthostatic intolerance, paresthesia, cognitive dysfunction, and multisystem manifestations. These symptoms significantly impacted her quality of life and functional capacity. She was diagnosed with ME/CFS in early 2023 and POTS later that year. The case highlights the complexity of post-COVID conditions (PCC), which are characterized by a diverse range of new, recurrent, or persistent symptoms affecting multiple organ systems. The report emphasizes the need for a multidisciplinary approach to diagnosis and management, as well as the challenges in identifying objective biomarkers for these conditions. The study also underscores the importance of continued research and clinical training to better address the long-term effects of SARS-CoV-2 infections.
Original source text (English)
Abstract The rise in post-infectious syndromes following COVID-19, such as Myalgic Encephalomyelitis/Chronic Fatigue Syndrome (ME/CFS) and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome (POTS), has become increasingly common in clinical practice, representing an emerging clinical challenge. This case highlights a possible clinical scenario involving the interaction between sequential viral infections - such as the Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) and Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) - in triggering overlapping chronic conditions. This report presents the case of a patient who developed ME/CFS and POTS following SARS-CoV-2 infection, with a prior history of EBV mononucleosis, and discusses the diagnostic, therapeutic, and pathophysiological implications. A 48-year-old woman with a history of mononucleosis in childhood developed persistent and disabling symptoms after COVID-19 in January 2022. She presented with extreme fatigue, orthostatic intolerance, paresthesia, cognitive dysfunction, and multisystem symptoms. After extensive investigation, she was diagnosed with ME/CFS (early 2023) and POTS (later that year). Mast Cell Activation Syndrome (MCAS) was considered as a hypothesis during follow-up, but objective testing was not confirmatory. Management included pharmacological therapy (including low-dose naltrexone, duloxetine, and palmitoylethanolamide (PEA)), activity adjustments (pacing), and a multidisciplinary approach, with partial and fluctuating responses. This case illustrates how viral infections such as EBV and SARS-CoV-2 may act as sequential triggers in an individual clinical trajectory compatible with the development of ME/CFS and POTS. The symptomatic overlap, the lack of readily available objective biomarkers in this patient, and the need for a predominantly clinical and functional diagnostic approach in her evaluation highlight the complexity of these conditions. This experience also underscores the importance of continued efforts in research, clinical training, and integrated care models to improve the management of patients presenting with post-COVID conditions (PCC). Introduction Myalgic Encephalomyelitis, also referred to as Chronic Fatigue Syndrome (ME/CFS), is a debilitating condition characterized by persistent and disabling fatigue without apparent cause, which does not improve with rest and worsens after physical or cognitive exertion. Post-exertional malaise (PEM) is a core symptom, often accompanied by cognitive impairment, musculoskeletal pain, and sleep disturbances, resulting in a significant impact on quality of life. The disease is more prevalent in women of reproductive age, with its main risk factors including previous viral infections (such as Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV) and Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2), positive family history, autoimmune diseases, intense physical or psychological stress, and associated chronic conditions such as fibromyalgia, Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome (POTS) [1,2]. On the other hand, POTS is a form of dysautonomia that frequently coexists with ME/CFS. It is characterized by orthostatic intolerance associated with an excessive increase in heart rate upon assuming an upright position, resulting in symptoms such as dizziness, palpitations, fatigue, and cognitive deficits. The overlap between ME/CFS and POTS is common and is associated with greater symptom intensity and worse clinical prognosis. Among the risk factors for POTS are a family history of dysautonomia, genetic predisposition, hormonal changes, and previous infections, such as mononucleosis (EBV), influenza, Lyme disease, and, more recently, COVID-19 [3-5]. These conditions share risk factors such as previous viral infections, genetic predisposition, and autoimmune diseases, suggesting an interaction between genetic susceptibility and environmental triggers [3-6]. Post-COVID conditions (PCC) are complex and multifaceted issues that arise after the acute phase of a SARS-CoV-2 infection. They are characterized by a diverse spectrum of new, recurrent, or persistent symptoms affecting multiple organ systems -such as neurological, cardiovascular, respiratory, and muscular - and which cannot be attributed to other diagnoses [7,8]. In addition to physical symptoms, studies report that many patients face intense post-pandemic emotional stress, exacerbated by uncertainty, isolation, and the burden of chronic illness [9,10]. There is also evidence that persistent inflammation may last for years after the initial infection, possibly contributing to prolonged symptomatology [11,12]. These symptoms typically manifest or persist for at least three months after the initial infection [7,8,13]. Beyond the physical impact, the condition carries profound mental, psychological, social, and economic consequences, including reduced work capacity, isolation, and diminished quality of life. Although its detailed pathophysiology remains under investigation, PCC represent a significant public health challenge, requiring a multidisciplinary approach to diagnosis, management, and support. Objective To report the case of a patient diagnosed with Myalgic Encephalomyelitis and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome who, following SARS-CoV-2 infection, developed persistent neurological, pulmonary, cardiac, cutaneous, and autonomic manifestations, resulting in significant functional impairment and reduced quality of life. Case Presentation The female patient had a history of six pregnancies, two deliveries, and four miscarriages. She was 48 years old, mixed-race, profession educator, and had a prior history of mononucleosis at age 11, hepatitis A at age 15, and an asthma diagnosis at age 20. The patient reported allergies to pineapple, kiwi, grapes, rosemary, oregano, eggplant, latex, shrimp, and other seafood. She experienced an episode of thrombophlebitis in her left upper limb at age 41. She denied any comorbidities prior to SARS-CoV-2 infection, as well as alcohol use and smoking. She engaged in regular physical exercise. The patient's clinical and temporal trajectory, from the acute SARS-CoV-2 infection through the evolution of PCC, diagnostic milestones, and therapeutic interventions, is detailed in Figure 1, which provides a graphical timeline of the case progression. The patient received the primary COVID-19 vaccination series with two doses of the Coronavac vaccine (SinoVac Biotech/Instituto Butantan) in early 2021. Subsequently, the first booster dose with the Comirnaty vaccine (Pfizer/BioNTech) was administered towards the end of the year followed by the second booster dose with the same vaccine in the middle of the next year. Thus, the patient completed the vaccination protocol, consisting of the primary series followed by two boosters. During the first consultation with our team on after two years at the Carlos Prates Health Center, the patient reported that her SARS-CoV-2 infection symptoms began more than two and half years earlier. They were initially mild and characterized by nasal congestion, malaise, and dry cough. COVID-19 was confirmed by RT-qPCR. Starting on the fourth day of symptoms, she experienced marked fatigue. During the acute phase, she used paracetamol and dipyrone sporadically. Due to the persistence of symptoms beyond 20 days, additional tests were requested, including a chest X-ray, which showed no changes compared to the initial presentation. At that time, corticosteroids and salbutamol were prescribed. New symptoms progressively emerged over the following weeks and months, including paresthesia in the lower and upper limbs, difficulty walking, sweating, cold extremities, bruising, urticaria, extreme physical and mental fatigue, insomnia, orthostatic intolerance, headache, digestive problems, shortness of breath while speaking and eating, intolerance to sound and light, low back pain, blurred vision, bladder pain, and dizziness. The patient sought specialized care in various fields, including Rheumatology, Neurology, Infectious Diseases, Pulmonology, Internal Medicine, Cardiology, Angiology, and Chinese Medicine. Most consultations were conducted in the private healthcare network, with respiratory rehabilitation carried out at the Sarah Kubitschek network in Belo Horizonte, Brazil. Several complementary tests were performed without detecting significant abnormalities, such as laboratory tests (renal and hepatic enzyme levels, electrolytes, serology for herpes virus, cytomegalovirus, and Epstein-Barr virus, as well as urinalysis), function and imaging studies (spirometry, electroneuromyography, and brain magnetic resonance imaging), and other investigations (stool tests, pituitary hormone levels, thyroid hormones, and vitamin levels), all with results within normal parameters. Alternative diagnoses were systematically excluded through laboratory, imaging, and functional evaluations, all within normal limits. The PCC that had persisted for over three years since the acute SARS-CoV-2 infection included orthostatic intolerance, persistent fatigue, memory impairment, paresthesia in the lower and upper limbs, sweating and cold extremities, blurred vision, sensory hypersensitivity, insomnia, low back pain, bladder pain, gastroesophageal reflux, reduced hearing, and hair loss. The patient received diagnoses of PCC, i.e., ME/CFS in early 2023 and POTS at the end of the year. The diagnosis of ME/CFS was established according to the Institute of Medicine (IOM) 2015 diagnostic criteria (Systemic Exertion Intolerance Disease, SEID) [14]. The patient fulfilled all core criteria: (1) substantial reduction in pre-illness levels of occupational, educational, social, or personal activities, persisting for more than six months; (2) post-exertional malaise (PEM), documented by symptom worsening after light walking (10 minutes/day); (3) unrefreshing sleep; and (4) cognitive impairment and orthostatic intolerance, both present. POTS was confirmed by a head-up tilt-table test performed at an external cardiology service. Based on the medical report, the protocol involved head-up tilt at 70° for a passive phase of approximately 10 minutes. The diagnostic criterion applied was a sustained heart rate increment of ≥30 bpm within 10 minutes of upright posture, in the absence of orthostatic hypotension, accompanied by reproduction of the patient's orthostatic symptoms. Resting heart rate measurements obtained during subsequent follow-up visits were recorded as vital signs and were not used to establish the diagnosis of POTS. Regarding work, she was on medical leave for 45 days in mid-2022, returning towards the end of the same year after symptom stabilization. A year later, due to worsening condition, she was again placed on medical leave. Her therapeutic regimen included pyridostigmine bromide 60 mg (every 12 hours), duloxetine hydrochloride 60 mg/day, naltrexone 2 mg/day, bupropion 300 mg/day, palmitoylethanolamide (PEA) 600 mg/day, vitamin C 1 g/day, and Omega-3. During the second consultation with our team another year later, the patient reported that a rheumatologist in Belo Horizonte, consulted two months earlier, had reduced duloxetine hydrochloride to 30 mg and increased PEA to 900 mg/day, and that she experienced worsening of symptoms after starting light walks (10 minutes/day). In the third consultation two months later, the patient reported another visit to the rheumatologist (end of the year), who increased PEA to 1200 mg/day, and to a psychiatrist (end of the year), who maintained bupropion 300 mg/day, naltrexone 2 mg/day, and duloxetine hydrochloride 30 mg/day. She reported partial improvement in fatigue, orthostatic symptoms, and memory, but worsening of hair loss. She discontinued acupuncture, started hydroxyzine hydrochloride 25 mg/day for gastric and post-exertional symptoms, and continued the walks. During the fourth consultation early next year, a worsening of her condition was observed following a trip, with increased physical and cognitive fatigue, reduced domestic and leisure activities, and more frequent flu-like symptoms. Mast Cell Activation Syndrome (MCAS) was considered as a diagnostic hypothesis, but no objective testing was available to confirm it; therefore, it remains speculative and unconfirmed. The patient consulted a new rheumatologist in São Paulo, Brazil, who requested tests and proposed pulse therapy, as well as an infectious disease specialist next month, who maintained the physiotherapy sessions. In the last consultation with our team during monitoring in mid-2025, the patient reported starting pulse therapy with methylprednisolone 125 mg/day for three days, initiated on five months earlier, and treatment with vitamin B12 1 mg/day two months earlier. The patient was prospectively evaluated and followed in person by the authors at our facility, and the information in this report was obtained during these consultations, complemented by review of medical records. Detailed data obtained during the clinical consultations and follow-up visits are provided in Table 1. These consultations were conducted as part of a clinical study called VITA Monitoring, which is responsible for following 212 individuals with PCC under the coordination of the Oswaldo Cruz Foundation and in partnership with the Brazilian Ministry of Health and the Belo Horizonte Municipal Health Department. The consultations took place at the Carlos Prates Health Center in Belo Horizonte, Minas Gerais, Brazil. VITA Monitoring is an 18-month cohort with five in-person consultations and telephone follow-ups, including clinical and laboratory analyses. This work reports follow-up data at multiple time points: baseline (V1) and evaluations after one month (1M), three months (3M), six months (6M), and twelve months (12M), conducted from mid-2024 onwards for a year. Written informed consent for publication of this case report and any accompanying images was obtained from the patient. Discussion This case illustrates the complex interaction between viral infections, individual predisposition, and the emergence of chronic overlapping syndromes such as ME/CFS and POTS. The patient had a significant history of prior EBV infection in childhood, a well-established risk factor for the development of post-infectious diseases. Subsequently, the acute SARS-CoV-2 infection acted as a potent secondary triggering event, generating a cascade of persistent and debilitating symptoms. This scenario supports the double-hit vulnerability model, in which an initial viral infection predisposes the immune and neurological systems, while a second insult triggers the full clinical syndrome. The high prevalence of ME/CFS and POTS in post-COVID cohorts reinforces the role of SARS-CoV-2 as a predominant trigger in the current era, suggesting common pathophysiological mechanisms such as autonomic dysfunction, neuroinflammation, and abnormal immune activation [15,16]. The patient's diagnostic journey, which involved multiple specialties and extensive evaluation through complementary tests with normal results, reflects one of the greatest challenges in managing ME/CFS and POTS: the difficulty of applying conventional tests. The absence of specific biochemical or imaging markers often leads to diagnostic delays, disbelief from some healthcare professionals, and additional suffering for the patient. This case highlights the crucial importance of established clinical criteria and functional tests, such as the Tilt Test for POTS and the assessment of PEM for ME/CFS. The diagnosis therefore remains grounded in careful history-taking and recognition of characteristic symptomatic patterns. This reinforces the urgent need for greater clinical training so that healthcare professionals understand that PCCs are real and diverse entities, and must be alert and sensitive to the profile of a significant portion of patients who, like the one described, present with severe illness in the absence of classic laboratory abnormalities. MCAS was considered as a possible unifying mechanism for the patient's multisystem symptoms, including urticaria, gastrointestinal complaints, intolerances, fatigue, and brain fog. However, no objective testing (serum tryptase, urinary histamine metabolites) was performed or confirmatory in this case. Therefore, MCAS remains an unconfirmed hypothesis and should be interpreted with caution. Growing evidence suggests an association between MCAS and PCC, ME/CFS, and POTS [17,18], but further objective testing is needed before drawing definitive conclusions. From a therapeutic standpoint, the management of ME/CFS and PCC with POTS is characterized by an empirical and multidisciplinary approach, largely guided by real-world evidence reported by patients. For POTS, this systematic review highlights interventions such as compression stockings and salt supplementation as first-line non-pharmacological approaches that improve symptoms and hemodynamics, while ivabradine and beta-adrenergic blockers (such as propranolol and bisoprolol) showed strong evidence among pharmacological options. In parallel, recent studies on ME/CFS highlight other interventions with consistently positive responses: pacing, considered the strategy with the highest perceived benefit, especially improves fatigue, PEM, and brain fog; low-dose naltrexone leading to significant results in the same symptoms, albeit with a limited effect on POTS; and intravenous or subcutaneous immunoglobulin (IgG) with broad benefits, including for fatigue, post-exertional malaise, POTS, and brain fog, particularly in patients with multisystem symptoms [16,19]. The prescribed medication regimen combined neuromodulators, an anti-inflammatory agent (PEA), and attempts at immune modulation (corticosteroid pulse therapy). It is important to note that the MCAS hypothesis opens a distinct pharmacological perspective. If confirmed, management could include targeted anti...

Time
The patient developed symptoms after SARS-CoV-2 infection in January 2022, with diagnosis of ME/CFS in early 2023 and POTS later that year. The publication date is 2026-09-24.
Location and population
The case was reported in a clinical setting, with the patient being a 48-year-old woman with a history of mononucleosis in childhood. The location is not specified in the evidence.
Event
A case of a patient who developed Myalgic Encephalomyelitis/Chronic Fatigue Syndrome (ME/CFS) and Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome (POTS) following SARS-CoV-2 infection, with a prior history of Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) mononucleosis. This is a confirmed clinical case reported by the authors of the article.
Scale, impact and risk
The patient experienced persistent and disabling symptoms, including extreme fatigue, orthostatic intolerance, paresthesia, cognitive dysfunction, and multisystem symptoms. These conditions significantly impacted her quality of life and functional capacity. The report does not provide specific case or death counts but highlights the complexity and severity of post-COVID conditions.
Response, status and uncertainty
The patient received a multidisciplinary approach to management, including pharmacological therapy and activity adjustments. The diagnosis was confirmed through clinical evaluation, and the case is presented as an example of the challenges in diagnosing and managing post-COVID conditions. The investigation was extensive, but no objective testing confirmed all hypotheses, including Mast Cell Activation Syndrome (MCAS).

Rivera-Mariani co-authors machine learning research on COVID-19 severity - Lynn University

Rivera-Mariani co-authors machine learning research on COVID-19 severity - Lynn University
新闻简报Félix Rivera-Mariani, an associate professor at Lynn University, co-authored a study published in the Journal of Immunology in August 2026. The research explores the use of machine learning to analyze plasma proteomics and single-cell immune data related to the severity of COVID-19. This work identifies potential markers for disease severity and proposes an approach that could be applied to future studies in environmental and viral immunology. The study was conducted at Lynn University, with contributions from alumni Tashoy Campbell '25 and Megan Ramsey '22, '26. While the findings are preliminary and align with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goal #3, no specific case, death, or exposure counts were reported. The research does not detail a public health response or risk assessment, but it highlights the potential of machine learning in understanding viral immunology.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究发表于2026年8月,新闻报道发表于2026年9月25日。
地点与对象
该研究是在林恩大学进行的,涉及校友 Tashoy Campbell '25 和 Megan Ramsey '22、'26。
事件
Félix Rivera-Mariani, an associate professor at Lynn University, co-authored a study on using machine learning to analyze plasma proteomics and single-cell immune data related to the severity of COVID-19.
规模、影响与风险
该研究确定了严重程度的潜在标志,并探索了一种可以扩展到环境和病毒免疫学未来研究的方法。没有报告具体病例、死亡或暴露计数。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是正在进行的学术探索的一部分,研究结果是初步的。该研究符合联合国可持续发展目标#3,但没有详细说明具体的公共卫生应对措施或风险评估。

2026–27 Flu, COVID-19 and RSV Vaccine Recommendations - Johns Hopkins Medicine

2026–27 Flu, COVID-19 and RSV Vaccine Recommendations - Johns Hopkins Medicine
新闻简报Johns Hopkins Medicine has released updated vaccine recommendations for the 2026–27 flu, COVID-19, and RSV seasons. The article emphasizes the importance of vaccination for most individuals aged 6 months and older to safeguard against seasonal illnesses. It outlines that flu season typically starts near the end of October and peaks between December and February, with the vaccine taking about two weeks to provide protection. The CDC recommends annual flu vaccines for people aged 6 months and older, with specific dosing requirements for children under 9 years old. A new mRNA flu vaccine (mFLUSIVA) is available for adults 50 and older. For COVID-19, the article states that vaccines are updated annually to target the most relevant strains and are recommended for all individuals 6 months and older. It highlights that vaccination is especially important for high-risk groups such as adults 65 and older, immunocompromised individuals, long-term-care residents, and pregnant women. The article also mentions that antiviral medications can be used for those who test positive for COVID-19 and are at increased risk for severe illness. Additionally, it notes that the RSV vaccine and antibody treatments are available for certain high-risk children and infants.

约翰霍普金斯大学医学,一般人群。

Johns Hopkins Medicine provides updated vaccine recommendations for flu, COVID-19, and RSV for the 2026–27 season.

应对、状态与不确定性
建议使用 疫苗接种 作为预防措施,治疗 可供感染者使用。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

2026–27 Flu, COVID-19 and RSV Vaccine Recommendations - Johns Hopkins Medicine

News Brief
Johns Hopkins Medicine has released updated vaccine recommendations for the 2026–27 flu, COVID-19, and RSV seasons. The article emphasizes the importance of vaccination for most individuals aged 6 months and older to safeguard against seasonal illnesses. It outlines that flu season typically starts near the end of October and peaks between December and February, with the vaccine taking about two weeks to provide protection. The CDC recommends annual flu vaccines for people aged 6 months and older, with specific dosing requirements for children under 9 years old. A new mRNA flu vaccine (mFLUSIVA) is available for adults 50 and older. For COVID-19, the article states that vaccines are updated annually to target the most relevant strains and are recommended for all individuals 6 months and older. It highlights that vaccination is especially important for high-risk groups such as adults 65 and older, immunocompromised individuals, long-term-care residents, and pregnant women. The article also mentions that antiviral medications can be used for those who test positive for COVID-19 and are at increased risk for severe illness. Additionally, it notes that the RSV vaccine and antibody treatments are available for certain high-risk children and infants.
Original source text (English)
Skip to Main Content Johns Hopkins Medicine Menu Health School of Medicine Johns Hopkins Medicine Home About Patient Care Research MyChart Schedule an Appointment Find a Doctor Find a Clinical Trial Pay Your Bill Employment Close Search Search Johns Hopkins Medicine Search Close MyChart Login MyChart Health Health Menu Health Main Menu Health Home Conditions and Diseases Treatments, Tests and Therapies Wellness and Prevention Caregiving Home Health Home Wellness and Prevention 2026–27 Flu, COVID-19 and RSV Vaccine Recommendations Infectious Diseases Influenza COVID-19 Published September 25, 2026 As the weather cools and people spend more time indoors, common viruses — including those that cause the flu, COVID-19 and respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) — have opportunities to spread and infect their hosts. To help people of all ages safeguard against seasonal illness, physicians and experts at Johns Hopkins Medicine share strategies to support optimal health. Key Points Flu season starts near the end of October, so it’s a good to get vaccinated soon (per guidelines below) for flu, COVID-19 and RSV. You can take steps to reduce the risk of infection. Treatments can relieve symptoms and shorten the duration of illness. Talk to your healthcare provider about specific questions or concerns. Flu (Influenza) Vaccine Recommendations The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends that most people age 6 months and older receive an annual flu vaccine. Vaccination before the end of October is ideal, but vaccination later in the season can still offer protection. Here are a few things to remember about the flu vaccine: It’s never too late: The flu vaccine takes about two weeks to help train your body how to recognize and respond to influenza, so it’s never too late to get an updated vaccine — you can do it at any time during the flu season, which tends to peak between December and February and can extend through May. What’s new: All flu vaccines for the 2026–27 season were designed to protect against three different influenza viruses: two influenza A viruses and an influenza B/Victoria virus. A newly approved mRNA flu vaccine (mFLUSIVA) is available for adults age 50 and older. Flu Vaccine Recommendation by Age Most people age 6 months and older should get an annual, age-appropriate flu vaccine: Children ages 6 months to 8 years should get one or two doses. Two doses are necessary for children: Who have never received a flu vaccine. Whose vaccine history is unknown. Who received the vaccine before but not according to the recommended schedule (two doses given at least four weeks apart). Children age 9 years and older should get one dose. Flu Vaccine Options When it comes to the flu, different vaccine options — including flu shots and a nasal spray — are available. Similar to other viruses, flu vaccine types and dosing vary based on a person’s age, their underlying health conditions and risks for severe illness, as well as personal preferences. Common flu vaccine options include: Egg- or cell-based flu shots (appropriate for many age groups). A new mRNA vaccine for adults age 50 and older. For adults age 65 and older, CDC recommends higher-dose flu vaccines (including high-dose inactivated and recombinant vaccines) or adjuvanted inactivated flu vaccine. A nasal spray flu vaccine is an option for people ages 2 through 49 who are not pregnant and who do not have underlying health conditions. What treatments are available if I get the flu? Antiviral medications are available for children and adults. These medicines work best when taken within two days of getting first symptoms. Over-the-counter medications can be used to treat pain and reduce fevers, but children and teens should take acetaminophen (not aspirin). People with severe illness and/or complications from the flu may need additional care, including breathing assistance, extra hydration and other medications to support recovery. Can I get flu and COVID vaccines at the same time? Yes, most people can safely get the flu and COVID-19 vaccines during the same visit. If you are getting shots for both, they may be given in different arms. As with any vaccine, you may experience temporary side effects such as soreness at the injection site, fatigue, headache and muscle aches. However, the side effects after getting both vaccines together are generally similar to those seen when the vaccines are given separately. And if you are eligible for the RSV vaccine, you can get all three during the same visit. If you have questions about the best timing based on your age and health conditions, talk with your healthcare provider. COVID-19 (SARS-CoV-2) Vaccine Recommendations COVID-19 vaccines help protect against severe illness, hospitalization and death. Each year, the COVID-19 vaccine is updated to target the most relevant strains of the virus. Johns Hopkins Medicine recommends all individuals 6 months and older receive the 2026–27 COVID-19 vaccine. This recommendation is based on assessments within our organization and review of medical data. Check with your healthcare provider if you have questions specific to your health needs. Vaccination is especially important for: Adults 65 and older. People at high risk for severe COVID-19, such as those who are immunocompromised. Long-term-care residents. People who have never been vaccinated against COVID-19. People who are pregnant or planning pregnancy. Children ages 6–23 months. COVID-19 Vaccine Options There are several COVID-19 vaccine options. People can choose from mRNA vaccines (e.g., Moderna or Pfizer-BioNTech) or a protein-based vaccine (e.g., Novavax), depending on the person’s age and vaccine availability: Moderna’s mRNA (Spikevax) vaccine can be given to anyone 6 months and over. Pfizer’s mRNA vaccine (Comirnaty) can be given to anyone 5 years and over. Moderna’s lower-dose mRNA vaccine (mNexspike) can be given to anyone 12 years and over. Novavax’s protein-based vaccine (Nuvaxovid) can be given to anyone 12 years and over. What treatments are available if I get COVID-19? Antiviral medications can help reduce the risk of severe illness, hospitalization and death in people who are at higher risk for complications from COVID-19. These treatments work best when started within five to seven days after symptoms begin. If you test positive for COVID-19 and are at increased risk for severe illness, contact your healthcare provider as soon as possible to discuss whether antiviral medications are right for you. People with severe illness and/or complications from COVID-19 may need additional care, including breathing assistance, extra hydration and other medications to support recovery. RSV (Respiratory Syncytial Virus) Vaccine Recommendations RSV vaccine recommendations vary by age: Women who are pregnant should get an RSV vaccine between 32 and 36 weeks of pregnancy to protect the baby after birth. If the mother did not get the RSV vaccine during pregnancy, if her vaccination status is unknown or if the baby was born within 14 days of the vaccination, the baby may need a long-acting RSV antibody (not a vaccine) before 8 months of age. Some children ages 8 to 19 months who are at an increased risk for severe RSV disease should receive the RSV antibody before their second RSV season (October through March). Adults age 75 and older should receive a single dose of an RSV vaccine. Adults ages 50–74 who are at increased risk for severe RSV disease should also receive a single dose. RSV vaccination is currently a one-time recommendation for eligible adults. People who have already received an RSV vaccine, even in a previous year, do not need another dose. What treatments are available if I get RSV? Most RSV symptoms are mild and should go away within a week or two. Over-the-counter medications can be used to treat pain and reduce fevers, but children and teens should take acetaminophen (not aspirin). People with severe illness and/or complications from RSV may need additional care, including breathing assistance, extra hydration and other medications to support recovery. Tips to Reduce Risk of Flu, COVID-19 or RSV Use good hand-washing practices, such as washing hands with soap and water for at least 20 seconds. Use hand sanitizer when soap and water aren’t an option. If possible, plan activities with large groups of people outdoors or in well-ventilated areas. Wear a well-fitted mask when in a crowded indoor setting or when around people who are sick. Sneeze or cough into your elbow when around other people. Stay away from people if you become sick, and avoid being around people who are sick, if possible. Stay hydrated, and prioritize getting adequate sleep to support immune function. Medically reviewed by Lisa Maragakis, M.D. Find a Doctor Specializing In: Covid-19 SARS-CoV-2 Find a Treatment Center Pediatric Infectious Diseases (Johns Hopkins Children's Center) Pediatric Infectious Diseases Program (Johns Hopkins All Children's Hospital) Find Additional Treatment Centers at: Howard County Medical Center Sibley Memorial Hospital Suburban Hospital Related Protecting Yourself and Others from Respiratory Viruses 11 Flu Myths Debunked: Infographic Flu: Answers from Physician Michael Albert Influenza (Flu) in Children Request an Appointment Find a Doctor 410-955-5000 Maryland 855-695-4872 Outside of Maryland +1-410-502-7683 International Find a Doctor See More Related Influenza Protecting Yourself and Others from Respiratory Viruses Influenza 11 Flu Myths Debunked: Infographic Infectious Diseases Flu: Answers from Physician Michael Albert Related Topics Infectious Diseases Influenza COVID-19
